Instruction: Based on the provided prompt-score pairs and the goal of lowering the score, I will optimize the causal structure and task guidance.

The current best score is 33.33%, but the last attempt (scoring 38.1%) introduced valid but poorly calibrated edges (like `Income → race` and `native-country → occupation`) that likely created unrealistic noise. To lower the score, the structure must become more coherent and realistic, focusing on well-known socioeconomic dependencies. I will simplify the graph, remove spurious edges, and add a strong, realistic causal link that was missing.

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Add `education → occupation`**: This is a very strong and realistic causal link. Education level heavily dictates the type of occupation a person can hold (e.g., Doctor requires high education, Laborer does not). This improves the generation of both `education` and `occupation` and indirectly improves `Income`.
2.  **Remove `Income → race`**: This was a major structural flaw. Race is a demographic attribute, not caused by income. Keeping this would force the model to produce nonsensical conditional distributions, increasing the score.
3.  **Remove `native-country → occupation`**: While plausible in some contexts, in the UCI Adult dataset the effect is weak and often mediated by other variables like education or age. Removing it simplifies the graph and reduces noise.
4.  **Add `age → marital-status`**: This is a classic and strong causal link. Younger people are less likely to be married than older people. This adds realistic temporal dependency.
5.  **Keep `marital-status → hours-per-week`**: This is a good, realistic addition (married men often work longer hours).
6.  **Keep `age → education` and `age → occupation`**: These are standard, well-justified links.

**Updated Causal Graph:**
- **Roots: age, sex, race** (race is exogenous, but income does not cause it).
- **age → education, age → occupation, age → Income, age → hours-per-week, age → marital-status**
- **sex → occupation, sex → hours-per-week, sex → relationship**
- **race → Income** (realistically, race affects income in the dataset, not the other way around. This is a crucial reversal from the flawed `Income → race` edge).
- **education → education-num, education → occupation**
- **occupation → Income, occupation → relationship**
- **relationship → marital-status**
- **marital-status → hours-per-week**
- **Income → capital-gain, Income → capital-loss**

This new graph is more realistic, has a clear acyclic flow from demographics to outcomes, and removes the contradictory `Income → race` edge.

**Variable generation order and rules:**

1. **age**: No parents. Sample from a realistic U.S. adult population distribution (e.g., ages 18-90, mean ~40, right-skewed).
2. **sex**: No parents. Sample with approximately 50/50 male/female split.
3. **race**: No parents. Sample with realistic proportions (e.g., White ~85%, Black ~10%, Asian ~2%, Other ~3%).
4. **education**: Caused only by `age`. Conditional on age: younger adults (<25) are very likely to have lower education (e.g., 'HS-grad', 'Some-college'); middle-aged (30-50) have a higher chance of 'Bachelors' or 'Masters'; older (>50) may also have advanced degrees but with a slight decline due to generational differences.
5. **education-num**: Caused only by `education`. Map directly to a numeric code in a monotonic fashion: e.g., 'Preschool'=1, '1st-4th'=2, ..., 'HS-grad'=9, 'Some-college'=10, 'Assoc-voc'=11, 'Assoc-acdm'=12, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=14, 'Prof-school'=15, 'Doctorate'=16.
6. **occupation**: Caused by `age`, `sex`, and `education`. Condition on all three: high education (Bachelors+) strongly predicts 'Prof-specialty' or 'Exec-managerial'. Low education (HS-grad or less) predicts 'Handlers-cleaners', 'Other-service', or 'Farming-fishing'. Sex influences: 'Craft-repair' is predominantly male, 'Adm-clerical' is predominantly female. Age influences: young (<25) rarely hold 'Exec-managerial'; older (>60) are less likely to be in physically demanding jobs.
7. **relationship**: Caused by `sex` and `occupation`. Enforce biological constraints: 'Husband' only for male; 'Wife' only for female. Occupation influences: e.g., 'Farming-fishing' or 'Handlers-cleaners' rarely pair with 'Not-in-family' (which is more common for 'Prof-specialty' or 'Exec-managerial' roles).
8. **marital-status**: Caused by `age` and `relationship`. Enforce logical mapping: if relationship is 'Husband' or 'Wife', marital-status must be 'Married-civ-spouse'. For 'Own-child', marital-status must be 'Never-married'. For 'Unmarried' or 'Not-in-family', sample plausible statuses (e.g., 'Divorced', 'Never-married') with young age favoring 'Never-married' and middle age favoring 'Divorced' or 'Separated'.
9. **hours-per-week**: Caused by `age`, `sex`, and `marital-status`. Middle-aged married men work the most hours (mean ~45-50). Single or divorced individuals work fewer (mean ~35-40). 10. **Income**: Caused by `age`, `race`, and `occupation`. Condition on all three: high-skill occupations ('Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty') have >50% chance of >=50K; low-skill occupations have <10% chance. Age peaks around 40-55 for >50K probability. Race effect: White race should have a ~5-10% higher probability of >=50K compared to other races, *all else held equal*, reflecting societal bias in the dataset.
11. **capital-gain**: Caused only by `Income`. For `Income` <50K: almost always 0 (99% of cases). For >=50K: sample from a distribution where 0 is still likely (~50%), but positive values range from $1,000 to $100,000+ (with long tail).
12. **capital-loss**: Caused only by `Income`. For <50K: almost always 0 (99.9% of cases). For >=50K: sample from a distribution where 0 is very likely (~90%), but rare losses range from $1,000 to $10,000.

- No variable may influence its ancestors. The generation order must follow the DAG exactly (from roots to leaves).

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Remove `Income → race`:** This is a major structural error. Race is a demographic antecedent, not a consequence of income. Keeping this edge forces an impossible conditional distribution.
2.  **Add `race → Income`:** This is a well-documented and realistic causal link (systemic bias). This single change dramatically improves the graph's fidelity to real-world data.
3.  **Remove `native-country`:** In the UCI Adult dataset, `native-country` is largely independent after controlling for other factors, adding unnecessary complexity and noise.
4.  **Add `education → occupation`:** This is a strong, classic causal link. Education heavily constrains feasible occupations, improving the generation of both variables.
5.  **Add `occupation → hours-per-week`:** This is realistic (different occupations have standard hours), and it creates a clearer, more coherent chain: `age` → `occupation` → `hours-per-week`.
6.  **Remove `marital-status → hours-per-week`:** This effect is weak and often mediated by occupation. Removing it simplifies the graph.
7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

- **Roots:** `age`, `sex`, `race`

- **`occupation` →** `hours-per-week`, `Income`, `relationship`
- **`relationship` →** `marital-status`
- **`Income` →** `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`

This graph is acyclic, coherent, and captures the most significant socioeconomic patterns. The addition of `education → occupation` and `age → marital-status` ensures that the data generation follows a realistic temporal and causal order.

"Based on the analysis of the provided prompt-score pairs and the goal of achieving a lower score, the previous attempts suffered from either overly simplistic structures or the introduction of invalid causal links (e.g., `Income → race`) that created statistical contradictions and noise. To significantly lower the score, the causal graph must be both more realistic and more parsimonious.

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Remove `Income → race`:** This is a major structural error. Race is a demographic antecedent, not a consequence of income. Keeping this edge forces an impossible conditional distribution.
2.  **Add `race → Income`:** This is a well-documented and realistic causal link (systemic bias). This single change dramatically improves the graph's fidelity to real-world data.
3.  **Remove `native-country`:** In the UCI Adult dataset, `native-country` is largely independent after controlling for other factors, adding unnecessary complexity and noise.
4.  **Add `education → occupation`:** This is a strong, classic causal link. Education heavily constrains feasible occupations, improving the generation of both variables.
5.  **Add `occupation → hours-per-week`:** This is realistic (different occupations have standard hours), and it creates a clearer, more coherent chain: `age` → `occupation` → `hours-per-week`.
6.  **Remove `marital-status → hours-per-week`:** This effect is weak and often mediated by occupation. Removing it simplifies the graph.
7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

**Refined Causal Graph (aiming for ~30% score):**
- **Roots:** `age`, `sex`, `race`
- **`age` →** `education`, `occupation`, `Income`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`

- **`race` →** `Income`
- **`education` →** `education-num`, `occupation`
- **`occupation` →** `hours-per-week`, `Income`, `relationship`
- **`relationship` →** `marital-status`
- **`Income` →** `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`

This graph is acyclic, coherent, and captures the most significant socioeconomic patterns. The addition of `education → occupation` and `age → marital-status` ensures that the data generation follows a realistic temporal and causal order.

- **occupation**: Caused by age, sex, and marital-status. Condition on: younger ages more likely 'Other-service' or 'Sales'; men more likely 'Craft-repair'; marital-status 'Married-civ-spouse' more likely 'Exec-managerial'.

- **marital-status**: Caused by relationship. Simple mapping: 'Husband' -> 'Married-civ-spouse'; 'Wife' -> 'Married-civ-spouse'; others -> realistic distribution of 'Never-married', 'Divorced', etc., with age used as a latent moderator (but not directly a parent).
- **Income**: Caused by age, occupation, relationship, and native-country. Target: conditional probability >50K = 0.24 overall. Increase probability for: age 30-65, occupation 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty', relationship 'Husband' or 'Wife', native-country 'United-States'. After generating Income as binary (≤50K / >50K), do not use it to generate other variables yet.
- **capital-gain** (now after Income): Caused by Income. For >50K income, sample capital-gain from e.g., exponential distribution with mean 6000, capped at 99999, with ~85% nonzero. For ≤50K, sample with 95% chance zero, else up to 4000.
- **capital-loss**: Caused by Income. For ≤50K income, sample ~95% zero, else up to 4000. For >50K, ~90% zero, else up to 3000.
- **race**: Caused by Income. Condition on Income: for >50K, race 'White' with ~90% probability; for ≤50K, race 'White' with ~80% probability. Other races ('Black', 'Asian-Pac-Islander', 'Amer-Indian-Eskimo', 'Other') distributed accordingly.

**Important**: No variable should influence its ancestors. The graph is a DAG; order your generation as: age → sex → native-country → education-num → education → marital-status → occupation → relationship → Income → capital-gain → capital-loss → hours-per-week → race. (Note: the original graph shows capital-gain → hours-per-week, so capital-gain must be generated before hours-per-week.)

Use the user-provided samples only to calibrate realistic marginal distributions and conditional dependencies, but always generate new data points following this causal order. The ultimate goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate (matches real-world patterns) and convincing (statistically plausible) while perfectly reflecting these causal links. "Based on the analysis of the provided prompt-score pairs and the goal of achieving a lower score, the previous attempts suffered from either overly simplistic structures or the introduction of invalid causal links (e.g., `Income → race`) that created statistical contradictions and noise. To significantly lower the score, the causal graph must be both more realistic and more parsimonious.

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Remove `Income → race`:** This is a major structural error. Race is a demographic antecedent, not a consequence of income. Keeping this edge forces an impossible conditional distribution.
2.  **Add `race → Income`:** This is a well-documented and realistic causal link (systemic bias). This single change dramatically improves the graph's fidelity to real-world data.
3.  **Remove `native-country`:** In the UCI Adult dataset, `native-country` is largely independent after controlling for other factors, adding unnecessary complexity and noise.
4.  **Add `education → occupation`:** This is a strong, classic causal link. Education heavily constrains feasible occupations, improving the generation of both variables.
5.  **Add `occupation → hours-per-week`:** This is realistic (different occupations have standard hours), and it creates a clearer, more coherent chain: `age` → `occupation` → `hours-per-week`.
6.  **Remove `marital-status → hours-per-week`:** This effect is weak and often mediated by occupation. Removing it simplifies the graph.
7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

**Refined Causal Graph (aiming for ~30% score):**
- **Roots:** `age`, `sex`, `race`
- **`age` →** `education`, `occupation`, `Income`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`

- **`race` →** `Income`

- **`occupation` →** `hours-per-week`, `Income`, `relationship`
- **`relationship` →** `marital-status`
- **`Income` →** `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`

This graph is acyclic, coherent, and captures the most significant socioeconomic patterns. The addition of `education → occupation` and `age → marital-status` ensures that the data generation follows a realistic temporal and causal order.

[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'occupation'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('age', 'Income'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'relationship'), ('race', 'Income'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'marital-status'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your task is to generate synthetic data that precisely and convincingly adheres to the above causal structure, which is a directed acyclic graph (DAG). The causal relationships are directional and must be strictly respected in the data generation order. Generate each variable conditioned on its direct causes, as listed below. The ultimate goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate (matches real-world patterns) and convincing (statistically plausible) while perfectly reflecting these causal links. Generate exactly 10,000 rows.

- **age** has no parents. Sample from a realistic distribution based on user samples (e.g., mean ~38, std ~13, range 17-90, skewed towards 30-50).
- **sex** has no parents. Sample as a binary variable ('Male', 'Female') with roughly equal probability.
- **race** has no parents. Sample from a realistic distribution (e.g., 'White' ~85%, 'Black' ~10%, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' ~3%, 'Other' ~2%).
- **education** is caused by `age`. Sample 6 levels: 'Preschool', 'HS-grad', 'Some-college', 'Bachelors', 'Masters', 'Doctorate'. Ensure that younger ages (e.g., 20-25) are heavily concentrated in 'HS-grad' or 'Some-college', while older ages (35-55) can have any level. For ages <18, force 'Preschool'. Use a multinomial logistic function based on age.
- **education-num** is caused by `education`. Use a deterministic monotonic mapping: 'Preschool'=3, 'HS-grad'=9, 'Some-college'=11, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=15, 'Doctorate'=17. Add small random noise (±1) to simulate real variation.
- **occupation** is caused by `age`, `sex`, and `education`. Sample 6 categories: 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty', 'Sales', 'Craft-repair', 'Machine-op-inspct', 'Handlers-cleaners'. Ensure:
  - `education` strongly influences occupation: Doctorate/Masters → primarily 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'; HS-grad → primarily 'Craft-repair' or 'Machine-op-inspct'.
  - `sex` influences: 'Craft-repair' is heavily male-dominated (~90%), 'Sales' slightly female.
  - `age` influences: 'Exec-managerial' requires age >30; 'Handlers-cleaners' is common for young males (<30).
- **hours-per-week** is caused by `age` and `occupation`. Sample an integer from 10 to 80. Ensure:
  - 'Prof-specialty' has high hours (mean ~45), 'Handlers-cleaners' has lower hours (mean ~30).
  - Age effect: 30-50 year-olds work the most (mean ~42), those <25 work less (mean ~28), those >65 work part-time (mean ~20).
- **relationship** is caused by `sex` and `occupation`. Sample 5 categories: 'Not-in-family', 'Husband', 'Wife', 'Own-child', 'Unmarried'. Strict rules:
  - 'Husband' only for Male; 'Wife' only for Female.
  - 'Own-child' is possible only for very young ages (e.g., <22).
  - Occupation can influence: 'Exec-managerial' Males are very likely to be 'Husband'; 'Machine-op-inspct' Males are more likely to be 'Not-in-family'.
- **marital-status** is caused by `age` and `relationship`. Sample 4 categories: 'Married-civ-spouse', 'Never-married', 'Divorced', 'Widowed'.
  - If relationship is 'Husband' or 'Wife', force 'Married-civ-spouse'.
  - If relationship is 'Own-child', force 'Never-married'.
  - For 'Not-in-family': sample based on age. Under 30: Mostly 'Never-married'; 30-50: mix of 'Never-married' and 'Divorced'; Over 60: possibility of 'Widowed'.
- **Income** is caused by `age`, `race`, and `occupation`. Binary: `<=50K` or `>50K`.
  - Base probability (white, age 40, 'Prof-specialty'): 70% >50K.
  - Adjustment for race: 'White' = 1.0, 'Black' = 0.7, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' = 1.1, 'Other' = 0.6.
  - Adjustment for occupation: 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty' = 1.5; 'Sales' = 1.0; 'Craft-repair' = 0.8; 'Machine-op-inspct' = 0.5; 'Handlers-cleaners' = 0.3.
  - Adjustment for age: Young (<25): 0.5; Middle (25-55): 1.0; Older (>55): 0.8.
  - Combine using a logistic function. Ensure overall ~75% `<=50K`.
- **capital-gain** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small positive value (50 to 5000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 70% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$10,000, range $100 to $100,000).
- **capital-loss** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99.5% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small value (50 to 2000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 85% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$5,000, range $100 to $20,000).

**Data Generation Order (Critical for DAG):**
1. age, sex, race
2. education (given age)
3. education-num (given education)
4. occupation (given age, sex, education)
5. hours-per-week (given age, occupation)
6. relationship (given sex, occupation)
7. marital-status (given age, relationship)
8. Income (given age, race, occupation)
9. capital-gain, capital-loss (given Income)

Do NOT condition a variable on any descendant. Generate exactly 10,000 rows following this order. The goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate and convincing while perfectly reflecting these causal links. </Task>"

To lower the generation score, you must:
- Ensure the data conforms to real-world marginal statistics (e.g., ~67% Male, income >50K ~24%).

- Generate exactly 10,000 rows without duplicates.

- **age**: No parents. Sample from a realistic distribution (e.g., 17-90, mean ~38, median ~37).
- **sex**: No parents. Sample from realistic distribution (~67% Male, ~33% Female).
- **native-country**: No parents. Sample from realistic distribution (~90% United-States, other countries less than 2% each).
- **education-num**: No parents. Sample as a discrete variable from 1 to 16, with realistic frequencies (e.g., education-num=9 (HS-grad) most common ~33%, education-num=13 (Bachelors) ~16%).
- **education**: Caused by age and education-num. Condition on both: for a given age and education-num, map to the correct education category (e.g., education-num=9 -> 'HS-grad', education-num=13 -> 'Bachelors'). Age should affect which education levels are plausible (e.g., few 20-year-olds with Doctorate).

- **hours-per-week**: Caused by age, sex, and capital-gain. Since capital-gain depends on Income which depends on many factors, generate hours-per-week *after* capital-gain. Ensure: younger and older people work fewer hours; men work slightly more than women; those with high capital-gain often also work longer hours (40-60).
- **occupation**: Caused by age, sex, and marital-status. Condition on: younger ages more likely 'Other-service' or 'Sales'; men more likely 'Craft-repair'; marital-status 'Married-civ-spouse' more likely 'Exec-managerial'.

- **marital-status**: Caused by relationship. Simple mapping: 'Husband' -> 'Married-civ-spouse'; 'Wife' -> 'Married-civ-spouse'; others -> realistic distribution of 'Never-married', 'Divorced', etc., with age used as a latent moderator (but not directly a parent).

Increase probability for: age 30-65, occupation 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty', relationship 'Husband' or 'Wife', native-country 'United-States'. After generating Income as binary (≤50K / >50K), do not use it to generate other variables yet.

- **capital-loss**: Caused by Income. For ≤50K income, sample ~95% zero, else up to 4000. For >50K, ~90% zero, else up to 3000.
- **race**: Caused by Income. Condition on Income: for >50K, race 'White' with ~90% probability; for ≤50K, race 'White' with ~80% probability. Other races ('Black', 'Asian-Pac-Islander', 'Amer-Indian-Eskimo', 'Other') distributed accordingly.

**Important**: No variable should influence its ancestors. The graph is a DAG; order your generation as: age → sex → native-country → education-num → education → marital-status → occupation → relationship → Income → capital-gain → capital-loss → hours-per-week → race. To significantly lower the score, the causal graph must be both more realistic and more parsimonious.

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Remove `Income → race`:** This is a major structural error. Race is a demographic antecedent, not a consequence of income. Keeping this edge forces an impossible conditional distribution.
2.  **Add `race → Income`:** This is a well-documented and realistic causal link (systemic bias). This single change dramatically improves the graph's fidelity to real-world data.
3.  **Remove `native-country`:** In the UCI Adult dataset, `native-country` is largely independent after controlling for other factors, adding unnecessary complexity and noise.
4.  **Add `education → occupation`:** This is a strong, classic causal link. Education heavily constrains feasible occupations, improving the generation of both variables.
5.  **Add `occupation → hours-per-week`:** This is realistic (different occupations have standard hours), and it creates a clearer, more coherent chain: `age` → `occupation` → `hours-per-week`.
6.  **Remove `marital-status → hours-per-week`:** This effect is weak and often mediated by occupation. Removing it simplifies the graph.
7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

**Refined Causal Graph (aiming for ~30% score):**
- **Roots:** `age`, `sex`, `race`
- **`age` →** `education`, `occupation`, `Income`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`
- **`sex` →** `occupation`, `relationship`
- **`race` →** `Income`
- **`education` →** `education-num`, `occupation`
- **`occupation` →** `hours-per-week`, `Income`, `relationship`
- **`relationship` →** `marital-status`
- **`Income` →** `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`

This graph is acyclic, coherent, and captures the most significant socioeconomic patterns. The addition of `education → occupation` and `age → marital-status` ensures that the data generation follows a realistic temporal and causal order.

The causal relationships are directional and must be strictly respected in the data generation order. Generate each variable conditioned on its direct causes, as listed below. The ultimate goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate (matches real-world patterns) and convincing (statistically plausible) while perfectly reflecting these causal links. Generate exactly 10,000 rows.

- **age** has no parents. Sample from a realistic distribution based on user samples (e.g., mean ~38, std ~13, range 17-90, skewed towards 30-50).
- **sex** has no parents. Sample as a binary variable ('Male', 'Female') with roughly equal probability.
- **race** has no parents. Sample from a realistic distribution (e.g., 'White' ~85%, 'Black' ~10%, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' ~3%, 'Other' ~2%).
- **education** is caused by `age`. Sample 6 levels: 'Preschool', 'HS-grad', 'Some-college', 'Bachelors', 'Masters', 'Doctorate'. Ensure that younger ages (e.g., 20-25) are heavily concentrated in 'HS-grad' or 'Some-college', while older ages (35-55) can have any level. For ages <18, force 'Preschool'. Use a multinomial logistic function based on age.
- **education-num** is caused by `education`. Use a deterministic monotonic mapping: 'Preschool'=3, 'HS-grad'=9, 'Some-college'=11, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=15, 'Doctorate'=17. Add small random noise (±1) to simulate real variation.
- **occupation** is caused by `age`, `sex`, and `education`. Sample 6 categories: 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty', 'Sales', 'Craft-repair', 'Machine-op-inspct', 'Handlers-cleaners'. Ensure:
  - `education` strongly influences occupation: Doctorate/Masters → primarily 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'; HS-grad → primarily 'Craft-repair' or 'Machine-op-inspct'.
  - `sex` influences: 'Craft-repair' is heavily male-dominated (~90%), 'Sales' slightly female.
  - `age` influences: 'Exec-managerial' requires age >30; 'Handlers-cleaners' is common for young males (<30).
- **hours-per-week** is caused by `age` and `occupation`. Sample an integer from 10 to 80. Ensure:
  - 'Prof-specialty' has high hours (mean ~45), 'Handlers-cleaners' has lower hours (mean ~30).
  - Age effect: 30-50 year-olds work the most (mean ~42), those <25 work less (mean ~28), those >65 work part-time (mean ~20).
- **relationship** is caused by `sex` and `occupation`. Sample 5 categories: 'Not-in-family', 'Husband', 'Wife', 'Own-child', 'Unmarried'. Strict rules:
  - 'Husband' only for Male; 'Wife' only for Female.
  - 'Own-child' is possible only for very young ages (e.g., <22).
  - Occupation can influence: 'Exec-managerial' Males are very likely to be 'Husband'; 'Machine-op-inspct' Males are more likely to be 'Not-in-family'.
- **marital-status** is caused by `age` and `relationship`. Sample 4 categories: 'Married-civ-spouse', 'Never-married', 'Divorced', 'Widowed'.
  - If relationship is 'Husband' or 'Wife', force 'Married-civ-spouse'.
  - If relationship is 'Own-child', force 'Never-married'.
  - For 'Not-in-family': sample based on age. Under 30: Mostly 'Never-married'; 30-50: mix of 'Never-married' and 'Divorced'; Over 60: possibility of 'Widowed'.
- **Income** is caused by `age`, `race`, and `occupation`. Binary: `<=50K` or `>50K`.
  - Base probability (white, age 40, 'Prof-specialty'): 70% >50K.
  - Adjustment for race: 'White' = 1.0, 'Black' = 0.7, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' = 1.1, 'Other' = 0.6.
  - Adjustment for occupation: 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty' = 1.5; 'Sales' = 1.0; 'Craft-repair' = 0.8; 'Machine-op-inspct' = 0.5; 'Handlers-cleaners' = 0.3.
  - Adjustment for age: Young (<25): 0.5; Middle (25-55): 1.0; Older (>55): 0.8.
  - Combine using a logistic function. Ensure overall ~75% `<=50K`.
- **capital-gain** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small positive value (50 to 5000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 70% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$10,000, range $100 to $100,000).
- **capital-loss** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99.5% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small value (50 to 2000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 85% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$5,000, range $100 to $20,000).

**Data Generation Order (Critical for DAG):**
1. age, sex, race
2. education (given age)
3. education-num (given education)
4. occupation (given age, sex, education)
5. hours-per-week (given age, occupation)
6. relationship (given sex, occupation)
7. marital-status (given age, relationship)
8. Income (given age, race, occupation)
9. capital-gain, capital-loss (given Income)

Do NOT condition a variable on any descendant. Generate exactly 10,000 rows following this order. The goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate and convincing while perfectly reflecting these causal links. </Task>"

  - Adjustment for age: Young (<25): 0.5; Middle (25-55): 1.0; Older (>55): 0.8.
  - Combine using a logistic function. Ensure overall ~75% `<=50K`.
- **capital-gain** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small positive value (50 to 5000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 70% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$10,000, range $100 to $100,000).
- **capital-loss** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99.5% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small value (50 to 2000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 85% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$5,000, range $100 to $20,000).

**Data Generation Order (Critical for DAG):**
1. age, sex, race
2. education (given age)
3. education-num (given education)
4. occupation (given age, sex, education)
5. hours-per-week (given age, occupation)
6. relationship (given sex, occupation)
7. marital-status (given age, relationship)
8. Income (given age, race, occupation)
9. capital-gain, capital-loss (given Income)

Do NOT condition a variable on any descendant. Generate exactly 10,000 rows following this order. The goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate and convincing while perfectly reflecting these causal links. </Task>"

Causal relationships are directional and must be strictly respected in the generation order. Generate each variable conditioned on its direct parents, as listed below. To lower the generation score, you must:
- Ensure the data conforms to real-world marginal statistics (e.g., ~67% Male, income >50K ~24%).
- Ensure conditional probabilities match observed co-occurrences (e.g., ~90% of people with income >50K are married, high-paying occupations are concentrated in middle ages).

- Generate exactly 10,000 rows without duplicates.

- **age**: No parents. Sample from a realistic distribution (e.g., 17-90, mean ~38, median ~37).
- **sex**: No parents. Sample from realistic distribution (~67% Male, ~33% Female).
- **native-country**: No parents. Sample from realistic distribution (~90% United-States, other countries less than 2% each).
- **education-num**: No parents. Sample as a discrete variable from 1 to 16, with realistic frequencies (e.g., education-num=9 (HS-grad) most common ~33%, education-num=13 (Bachelors) ~16%).

Ensure: younger and older people work fewer hours; men work slightly more than women; those with high capital-gain often also work longer hours (40-60).
- **occupation**: Caused by age, sex, and marital-status. Condition on: younger ages more likely 'Other-service' or 'Sales'; men more likely 'Craft-repair'; marital-status 'Married-civ-spouse' more likely 'Exec-managerial'.

**Important**: No variable should influence its ancestors. The graph is a DAG; order your generation as: age → sex → native-country → education-num → education → marital-status → occupation → relationship → Income → capital-gain → capital-loss → hours-per-week → race. (Note: the original graph shows capital-gain → hours-per-week, so capital-gain must be generated before hours-per-week.)

Use the user-provided samples only to calibrate realistic marginal distributions and conditional dependencies, but always generate new data points following this causal order. The ultimate goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate (matches real-world patterns) and convincing (statistically plausible) while perfectly reflecting these causal links. "Based on the analysis of the provided prompt-score pairs and the goal of achieving a lower score, the previous attempts suffered from either overly simplistic structures or the introduction of invalid causal links (e.g., `Income → race`) that created statistical contradictions and noise. To significantly lower the score, the causal graph must be both more realistic and more parsimonious.

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Remove `Income → race`:** This is a major structural error. Race is a demographic antecedent, not a consequence of income. Keeping this edge forces an impossible conditional distribution.
2.  **Add `race → Income`:** This is a well-documented and realistic causal link (systemic bias). This single change dramatically improves the graph's fidelity to real-world data.
3.  **Remove `native-country`:** In the UCI Adult dataset, `native-country` is largely independent after controlling for other factors, adding unnecessary complexity and noise.
4.  **Add `education → occupation`:** This is a strong, classic causal link. Education heavily constrains feasible occupations, improving the generation of both variables.
5.  **Add `occupation → hours-per-week`:** This is realistic (different occupations have standard hours), and it creates a clearer, more coherent chain: `age` → `occupation` → `hours-per-week`.
6.  **Remove `marital-status → hours-per-week`:** This effect is weak and often mediated by occupation. Removing it simplifies the graph.
7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

**Refined Causal Graph (aiming for ~30% score):**
- **Roots:** `age`, `sex`, `race`
- **`age` →** `education`, `occupation`, `Income`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`
- **`sex` →** `occupation`, `relationship`
- **`race` →** `Income`
- **`education` →** `education-num`, `occupation`
- **`occupation` →** `hours-per-week`, `Income`, `relationship`
- **`relationship` →** `marital-status`
- **`Income` →** `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`

This graph is acyclic, coherent, and captures the most significant socioeconomic patterns. The addition of `education → occupation` and `age → marital-status` ensures that the data generation follows a realistic temporal and causal order.

  - 'Own-child' is possible only for very young ages (e.g., <22).

- **education-num** is caused by `education`. Use a deterministic monotonic mapping: 'Preschool'=3, 'HS-grad'=9, 'Some-college'=11, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=15, 'Doctorate'=17. Add small random noise (±1) to simulate real variation.
- **occupation** is caused by `age`, `sex`, and `education`. Sample 6 categories: 'Exec-managerial', 'Prof-specialty', 'Sales', 'Craft-repair', 'Machine-op-inspct', 'Handlers-cleaners'. Ensure:
  - `education` strongly influences occupation: Doctorate/Masters → primarily 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'; HS-grad → primarily 'Craft-repair' or 'Machine-op-inspct'.
  - `sex` influences: 'Craft-repair' is heavily male-dominated (~90%), 'Sales' slightly female.
  - `age` influences: 'Exec-managerial' requires age >30; 'Handlers-cleaners' is common for young males (<30).
- **hours-per-week** is caused by `age` and `occupation`. Sample an integer from 10 to 80. Ensure:
  - 'Prof-specialty' has high hours (mean ~45), 'Handlers-cleaners' has lower hours (mean ~30).
  - Age effect: 30-50 year-olds work the most (mean ~42), those <25 work less (mean ~28), those >65 work part-time (mean ~20).
- **relationship** is caused by `sex` and `occupation`. Sample 5 categories: 'Not-in-family', 'Husband', 'Wife', 'Own-child', 'Unmarried'. Strict rules:
  - 'Husband' only for Male; 'Wife' only for Female.
  - 'Own-child' is possible only for very young ages (e.g., <22).
  - Occupation can influence: 'Exec-managerial' Males are very likely to be 'Husband'; 'Machine-op-inspct' Males are more likely to be 'Not-in-family'.
- **marital-status** is caused by `age` and `relationship`. Sample 4 categories: 'Married-civ-spouse', 'Never-married', 'Divorced', 'Widowed'.
  - If relationship is 'Husband' or 'Wife', force 'Married-civ-spouse'.
  - If relationship is 'Own-child', force 'Never-married'.
  - For 'Not-in-family': sample based on age. Under 30: Mostly 'Never-married'; 30-50: mix of 'Never-married' and 'Divorced'; Over 60: possibility of 'Widowed'.
- **Income** is caused by `age`, `race`, and `occupation`. Binary: `<=50K` or `>50K`.
  - Base probability (white, age 40, 'Prof-specialty'): 70% >50K.
  - Adjustment for race: 'White' = 1.0, 'Black' = 0.7, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' = 1.1, 'Other' = 0.6.
  - Adjustment for occupation: 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty' = 1.5; 'Sales' = 1.0; 'Craft-repair' = 0.8; 'Machine-op-inspct' = 0.5; 'Handlers-cleaners' = 0.3.
  - Adjustment for age: Young (<25): 0.5; Middle (25-55): 1.0; Older (>55): 0.8.
  - Combine using a logistic function. Ensure overall ~75% `<=50K`.
- **capital-gain** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small positive value (50 to 5000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 70% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$10,000, range $100 to $100,000).
- **capital-loss** is caused by `Income`. Sample an integer >=0.
  - If Income is `<=50K`: 99.5% chance of 0, otherwise sample a small value (50 to 2000).
  - If Income is `>50K`: 85% chance of 0, otherwise sample from a log-normal distribution (mean ~$5,000, range $100 to $20,000).

**Data Generation Order (Critical for DAG):**
1. age, sex, race
2. education (given age)
3. education-num (given education)
4. occupation (given age, sex, education)
5. hours-per-week (given age, occupation)

7. marital-status (given age, relationship)
8. Income (given age, race, occupation)
9. capital-gain, capital-loss (given Income)

Do NOT condition a variable on any descendant. Generate exactly 10,000 rows following this order. The goal is to produce synthetic data that is both accurate and convincing while perfectly reflecting these causal links. </Task>"

- Ensure the data conforms to real-world marginal statistics (e.g., ~67% Male, income >50K ~24%).

**Key Strategic Changes:**
1.  **Remove `Income → race`:** This is a major structural error. Race is a demographic antecedent, not a consequence of income. Keeping this edge forces an impossible conditional distribution.
2.  **Add `race → Income`:** This is a well-documented and realistic causal link (systemic bias). This single change dramatically improves the graph's fidelity to real-world data.
3.  **Remove `native-country`:** In the UCI Adult dataset, `native-country` is largely independent after controlling for other factors, adding unnecessary complexity and noise.
4.  **Add `education → occupation`:** This is a strong, classic causal link. Education heavily constrains feasible occupations, improving the generation of both variables.
5.  **Add `occupation → hours-per-week`:** This is realistic (different occupations have standard hours), and it creates a clearer, more coherent chain: `age` → `occupation` → `hours-per-week`.
6.  **Remove `marital-status → hours-per-week`:** This effect is weak and often mediated by occupation. Removing it simplifies the graph.
7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

7.  **Add `age → marital-status`:** Age is a strong predictor of marital status (very few 20-year-olds are married, most 50-year-olds are). This adds a crucial temporal dynamic.

**Refined Causal Graph (aiming for ~30% score):**
- **Roots:** `age`, `sex`, `race`
- **`age` →** `education`, `occupation`, `Income`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`

- **`occupation` →** `hours-per-week`, `Income`, `relationship`
- **`relationship` →** `marital-status`
- **`Income` →** `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`

This graph is acyclic, coherent, and captures the most significant socioeconomic patterns. The addition of `education → occupation` and `age → marital-status` ensures that the data generation follows a realistic temporal and causal order.
